### Question 122: Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia Type 2B (MEN 2B): RET M918T Mutation & Phenotype
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2B (MEN 2B), its characteristic clinical features (medullary thyroid carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, mucosal neuromas, Marfanoid habitus), its underlying genetic cause (specific RET proto-oncogene mutations), and its distinction from other MEN syndromes, particularly MEN 2A.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 12-year-old male presents with a family history of thyroid cancer. He is notably tall and slender with elongated limbs, described as having a Marfanoid habitus. Physical examination reveals multiple bumpy, fleshy nodules on his lips and anterior tongue. He reports severe chronic constipation since infancy. Neck ultrasound shows a large, aggressive medullary thyroid carcinoma with cervical lymph node metastases. Biochemical testing reveals elevated plasma free metanephrines, confirming the presence of an adrenal pheochromocytoma. Genetic testing identifies a heterozygous mutation in the RET proto-oncogene. Which of the following genetic mutations is most likely responsible for this patient's constellation of findings?

(A) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon C634R (exon 10)
(B) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon M918T (exon 16)
(C) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon Y884H (exon 15)
(D) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon D835A (exon 14)
(E) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon L759S (exon 13)

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Age and Presentation:** 12-year-old male, suggesting a genetic syndrome manifesting in childhood.
- **Marfanoid Habitus:** Tall stature, slender build, elongated limbs. This is a characteristic feature of MEN 2B.
- **Mucosal Neuromas:** Bumpy, fleshy nodules on lips and tongue. This is pathognomonic for MEN 2B.
- **Chronic Constipation:** Suggests intestinal ganglioneuromatosis, another key feature of MEN 2B.
- **Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma (MTC):** Aggressive MTC with metastases. MTC is a hallmark of MEN 2 syndromes (MEN 2A and MEN 2B).
- **Pheochromocytoma:** Elevated plasma free metanephrines confirm this. Pheochromocytoma is also a hallmark of MEN 2 syndromes.
- **Genetic Testing:** Confirms a mutation in the RET proto-oncogene.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the specific RET mutation most likely responsible for this clinical picture.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **MEN Syndromes & RET:** Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN 2) syndromes are autosomal dominant disorders caused by germline activating mutations in the RET proto-oncogene, which encodes a receptor tyrosine kinase (RTK). The RET protein is involved in cell growth, differentiation, and survival. Activating mutations lead to constitutive activation of the RET kinase, promoting uncontrolled cell proliferation in target tissues (thyroid C-cells, adrenal medulla chromaffin cells, intestinal ganglion cells).
- **MEN 2A vs. MEN 2B:** MEN 2 syndromes are subdivided into MEN 2A and MEN 2B based on the specific RET mutation and the resulting clinical phenotype.
    - **MEN 2A:** Typically caused by mutations in exons 10 and 11 of the RET gene (e.g., C634R, C634Y, C634W, M918T, D835A, Y884H). Characterized by MTC (usually less aggressive than in MEN 2B), pheochromocytoma, and primary hyperparathyroidism (due to parathyroid hyperplasia/adenoma). Mucosal neuromas and Marfanoid habitus are absent.
    - **MEN 2B:** Almost exclusively caused by a specific mutation in exon 16 of the RET gene, resulting in a methionine-to-threonine substitution at codon 918 (M918T). Characterized by early-onset, aggressive MTC, pheochromocytoma, mucosal neuromas (lips, tongue, conjunctiva, GI tract), intestinal ganglioneuromatosis (causing constipation/megacolon), skeletal abnormalities (Marfanoid habitus), and sometimes cutaneous lichen amyloidosis. Hyperparathyroidism is typically absent.
- **RET Mutations & Mechanism:** The RET protein consists of an extracellular ligand-binding domain, a single transmembrane domain, and an intracellular tyrosine kinase domain. Activating mutations in MEN 2 syndromes typically occur in the kinase domain (exons 10-16) or the ligand-binding domain (exon 14). The M918T mutation in MEN 2B is located in the kinase domain and causes a significant increase in the constitutive kinase activity of the RET protein, leading to more aggressive tumor development, particularly in the thyroid.
- **Catecholamine Synthesis & Pheochromocytoma:** Pheochromocytomas arise from chromaffin cells of the adrenal medulla (or extra-adrenal paraganglia). These cells synthesize and secrete catecholamines (epinephrine, norepinephrine, dopamine). The synthesis pathway involves tyrosine hydroxylase (rate-limiting), dopa decarboxylase, dopamine beta-hydroxylase, and phenylethanolamine N-methyltransferase (PNMT). Elevated plasma free metanephrines (metanephrine and normetanephrine) are sensitive markers for pheochromocytoma, reflecting continuous intratumoral catecholamine metabolism independent of episodic secretion.
- **Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma:** MTC arises from the parafollicular C-cells of the thyroid, which produce calcitonin. In MEN 2 syndromes, activating RET mutations cause these C-cells to proliferate uncontrollably.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  **Identify Key Features:** The patient presents with Marfanoid habitus, mucosal neuromas, chronic constipation (suggesting intestinal ganglioneuromatosis), aggressive MTC, and pheochromocytoma.
2.  **Recognize Syndrome:** This constellation of findings is highly characteristic of Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2B (MEN 2B).
3.  **Recall MEN 2B Genetics:** MEN 2B is almost always caused by a specific activating mutation in the RET proto-oncogene.
4.  **Differentiate MEN 2A/B Mutations:** MEN 2A is associated with mutations primarily in exons 10 and 11 (e.g., C634R, D835A, Y884H), while MEN 2B is almost exclusively associated with the M918T mutation in exon 16.
5.  **Match Mutation to Phenotype:** The patient's phenotype (Marfanoid habitus, mucosal neuromas, intestinal ganglioneuromatosis, aggressive MTC, pheochromocytoma) perfectly matches MEN 2B.
6.  **Select Correct Option:** Therefore, the most likely RET mutation is M918T (codon 918 in exon 16).

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon C634R (exon 10):** This mutation is commonly associated with MEN 2A, not MEN 2B. MEN 2A typically involves MTC, pheochromocytoma, and hyperparathyroidism, but lacks the characteristic features of MEN 2B (mucosal neuromas, Marfanoid habitus, intestinal ganglioneuromatosis).
- **(C) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon Y884H (exon 15):** This mutation is also associated with MEN 2A. Similar to C634R, it causes MTC and pheochromocytoma, often with hyperparathyroidism, but not the MEN 2B phenotype.
- **(D) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon D835A (exon 14):** This mutation is associated with MEN 2A, often presenting with a milder phenotype than C634R. It causes MTC, pheochromocytoma, and hyperparathyroidism, but not MEN 2B features.
- **(E) RET proto-oncogene mutation at codon L759S (exon 13):** This mutation is associated with Familial Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma (FMTC), a condition characterized by MTC only, without pheochromocytoma or hyperparathyroidism. It is not associated with MEN 2B.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature                 | MEN 2A                                  | MEN 2B                                                                 |
| :---------------------- | :-------------------------------------- | :--------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| **RET Mutation Location** | Exons 10, 11, 13, 14, 15               | Exon 16                                                                |
| **Common Mutations**    | C634R, C634Y, D835A, Y884H, L759S (FMTC) | M918T                                                                  |
| **Medullary Thyroid Ca** | Yes (usually less aggressive)           | Yes (early onset, aggressive)                                          |
| **Pheochromocytoma**     | Yes                                     | Yes                                                                    |
| **Hyperparathyroidism**  | Yes (common)                            | No                                                                     |
| **Mucosal Neuromas**     | No                                      | Yes (lips, tongue, conjunctiva, GI tract)                               |
| **Marfanoid Habitus**    | No                                      | Yes (tall, slender, long limbs)                                        |
| **Intestinal Ganglioneuromatosis** | No | Yes (chronic constipation/megacolon) |
| **Cutaneous Lichen Amyloidosis** | No | Yes |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
MEN 2B is a distinct genetic syndrome caused almost exclusively by the RET M918T mutation, characterized by aggressive medullary thyroid carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, mucosal neuromas, Marfanoid habitus, and intestinal ganglioneuromatosis. It is crucial to differentiate MEN 2B from MEN 2A based on the specific clinical features and the underlying RET mutation location (exon 16 for MEN 2B vs. primarily exons 10/11 for MEN 2A).

### Question 123: Timing of Prophylactic Thyroidectomy in MEN 2 Carriers
- **Difficulty**: Medium
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The timing of prophylactic thyroidectomy in patients with Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN 2) is determined by the specific RET proto-oncogene mutation, which dictates the risk stratification for early-onset medullary thyroid carcinoma (MTC). MEN 2B mutations, particularly M918T, confer the highest risk (HST) and necessitate surgery in early infancy.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old pregnant woman with a known history of Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2B (MEN 2B), confirmed by genetic testing showing the RET M918T mutation, delivers a male infant via uncomplicated vaginal delivery. Cord blood genetic testing confirms the infant has inherited the same RET M918T mutation. The infant is otherwise healthy at birth, with normal vital signs, physical examination, and initial newborn screening results. The pediatric endocrine team reviews the infant's case and recommends total thyroidectomy before the child reaches 1 year of age. Based on current guidelines for managing hereditary MTC, what is the primary rationale for this recommendation?

(A) The RET M918T mutation is associated with a moderate risk of MTC, typically developing after age 5, allowing for surveillance with serum calcitonin and CEA measurements until age 5.
(B) The RET M918T mutation confers a high risk of MTC, which characteristically develops rapidly in early infancy, often before age 1, with aggressive metastatic potential, necessitating prophylactic surgery within the first year of life.
(C) Prophylactic thyroidectomy is recommended for all MEN 2 carriers regardless of the specific RET mutation, typically performed between ages 5 and 10 to minimize surgical complications and maximize growth potential.
(D) The RET M918T mutation is associated with MEN 2A, which carries a lower risk of MTC compared to MEN 2B, allowing for surveillance until calcitonin levels rise significantly, usually after age 5.
(E) The infant's age at birth is the primary determinant for prophylactic thyroidectomy timing in MEN 2B, with surgery typically delayed until the child is at least 5 years old to reduce anesthetic risks and improve surgical outcomes.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** Newborn male infant.
- **Family History:** Mother with MEN 2B (RET M918T mutation).
- **Infant's Genetic Status:** Confirmed carrier of RET M918T mutation.
- **Recommendation:** Total thyroidectomy before age 1.
- **Question:** Rationale for this timing.

The key clues are the diagnosis of MEN 2B, the specific RET mutation (M918T), and the recommended timing of prophylactic thyroidectomy (before age 1). This points towards the high-risk nature of MTC associated with this specific mutation.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **MEN 2 Syndromes:** Autosomal dominant disorders caused by activating mutations in the RET proto-oncogene, a receptor tyrosine kinase involved in cell growth and differentiation.
- **RET Mutations & Risk Stratification:** Different RET mutations confer varying risks for MTC development and aggressiveness. The American Thyroid Association (ATA) guidelines stratify risk based on genotype:
    - **High-Risk (HST):** Mutations like M918T (MEN 2B) and certain MEN 2A mutations (e.g., codon 634). MTC typically develops very early in infancy (often within the first year), is aggressive, and metastasizes rapidly. Prophylactic thyroidectomy is recommended within the first year of life.
    - **Intermediate-Risk:** Mutations like A620V, P660R, S896F, Y903C, L836E (MEN 2A). MTC typically develops in early childhood (before age 5). Prophylactic thyroidectomy is recommended before age 5.
    - **Low-Risk (Surveillance):** Mutations like C634R, C634Y, C634W, V812M (MEN 2A). MTC typically develops later in childhood or adulthood. Surveillance with serum calcitonin and CEA measurements is recommended, with surgery performed if levels rise significantly.
- **MEN 2B:** Characterized by MTC (almost 100% penetrance, very early onset), pheochromocytoma (50%), mucosal neuromas, marfanoid habitus, and intestinal ganglioneuromatosis. The M918T mutation is the most common cause of MEN 2B.
- **MTC Pathophysiology:** MTC arises from parafollicular C-cells of the thyroid, which produce calcitonin. Activating RET mutations lead to constitutive activation of the RET tyrosine kinase, driving uncontrolled C-cell proliferation and tumor formation.
- **Rationale for Early Surgery:** In high-risk mutations (like M918T), MTC develops extremely early and aggressively. Prophylactic thyroidectomy before the tumor becomes established and metastasizes is curative. Delaying surgery increases the risk of lymph node and distant metastases, significantly worsening prognosis.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient is a newborn with MEN 2B due to inheriting the RET M918T mutation.
2. MEN 2B, particularly with the M918T mutation, is classified as a high-risk (HST) genotype for MTC according to ATA guidelines.
3. High-risk genotypes are associated with very early onset (often within the first year of life) and aggressive MTC.
4. Current guidelines recommend prophylactic total thyroidectomy for infants with high-risk MEN 2 mutations within the first year of life to prevent the development and spread of MTC.
5. Therefore, the recommendation for thyroidectomy before age 1 is based on the high risk associated with the RET M918T mutation.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Incorrect:** The RET M918T mutation is associated with *high* risk, not moderate risk. MTC develops very early, not typically after age 5. Surveillance is inappropriate for this high-risk genotype.
- **(C) Incorrect:** While prophylactic thyroidectomy is recommended for MEN 2 carriers, the timing depends on the risk stratification. Surgery is *not* typically delayed until ages 5-10; it's performed much earlier for high-risk mutations.
- **(D) Incorrect:** The RET M918T mutation is associated with MEN 2B, not MEN 2A. MEN 2B carries a higher risk of early-onset MTC than most MEN 2A mutations.
- **(E) Incorrect:** The infant's age at birth is the *reason* for immediate consideration of surgery, but the *primary rationale* is the high risk conferred by the specific mutation (M918T), which dictates the need for very early surgery, not a delay until age 5. The urgency is driven by the mutation's risk profile.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| MEN 2 Type | Common RET Mutations | Risk Stratification | Typical MTC Onset | Recommended Surgery Timing | Other Features |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MEN 2A | C634R, C634Y, C634W, V812M | Low/Intermediate | Childhood/Adulthood | Surveillance or before age 5 | Pheochromocytoma (50%), Parathyroid hyperplasia (90%) |
| MEN 2B | M918T | High | Infancy (often <1 yr) | Within first year of life | Pheochromocytoma (50%), Mucosal neuromas, Marfanoid habitus, Ganglioneuromatosis |
| FMTC | Various (often MTC-specific) | Variable | Childhood/Adulthood | Based on family history/risk | Medullary thyroid carcinoma only |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
The timing of prophylactic thyroidectomy in MEN 2 syndromes is dictated by the specific RET mutation and the associated risk of early-onset medullary thyroid carcinoma. High-risk mutations, such as RET M918T found in MEN 2B, necessitate surgery within the first year of life to prevent aggressive, early-stage MTC and improve long-term survival. Understanding the genotype-phenotype correlation is crucial for appropriate management.

### Question 124: Von Hippel-Lindau (VHL) Disease: Pseudohypoxia & Chromaffin Tumors
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: The question tests the understanding of the molecular pathophysiology of Von Hippel-Lindau (VHL) disease, specifically how loss of VHL function leads to constitutive activation of the HIF pathway, resulting in pseudohypoxia, angiogenesis, and the development of characteristic tumors including pheochromocytomas.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 26-year-old male presents to the clinic complaining of worsening headaches and blurred vision over the past 6 months. His past medical history is unremarkable, and he denies any significant family history of cancer or endocrine disorders. Physical examination reveals mild hypertension (155/90 mmHg) and bilateral retinal angiomas noted during fundoscopy. Neurological examination is otherwise normal. Brain MRI shows a 3 cm cystic lesion with an enhancing mural nodule in the cerebellum, consistent with a hemangioblastoma. Abdominal CT scan reveals bilateral, solid adrenal masses and multiple enhancing lesions in both kidneys, suggestive of clear cell renal cell carcinoma. Genetic testing confirms a heterozygous mutation in the VHL gene located on chromosome 3p25. Plasma free metanephrines are elevated at 3.5 ng/mL (normal <0.6 ng/mL). Which of the following molecular mechanisms best explains the development of pheochromocytomas in this patient?

(A) Loss of the pVHL E3 ubiquitin ligase complex leads to stabilization and accumulation of hypoxia-inducible factor 1-alpha (HIF-1α), promoting angiogenesis via increased vascular endothelial growth factor (VEGF) expression and catecholamine synthesis via increased expression of tyrosine hydroxylase (TH).
(B) The VHL mutation causes constitutive activation of the PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway, leading to increased cell proliferation and survival in chromaffin cells, independent of oxygen levels.
(C) Loss of VHL function results in decreased expression of the von Hippel-Lindau protein (pVHL), which normally inhibits the activity of tyrosine hydroxylase (TH), the rate-limiting enzyme in catecholamine synthesis.
(D) The VHL mutation leads to increased expression of the RET proto-oncogene, causing constitutive activation of the MAPK pathway and promoting the proliferation of chromaffin cells.
(E) Loss of pVHL function impairs the degradation of catecholamines within chromaffin cells, leading to increased intracellular levels and subsequent tumor formation.

#### Correct Answer: A

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** 26-year-old male with headaches, blurred vision, hypertension. These are non-specific but can be associated with CNS tumors (hemangioblastoma) and endocrine tumors (pheochromocytoma).
- **Physical Exam:** Retinal angiomas are characteristic of VHL disease. Hypertension is common in VHL due to renal cell carcinoma and pheochromocytoma.
- **Imaging:** Cerebellar hemangioblastoma, retinal angiomas, bilateral adrenal masses (pheochromocytomas), and clear cell renal cell carcinomas are the classic triad of VHL disease manifestations.
- **Genetics:** Confirmed VHL gene mutation on chromosome 3p25 confirms the diagnosis of VHL disease.
- **Labs:** Elevated plasma free metanephrines confirm the presence of a catecholamine-secreting tumor (pheochromocytoma).
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the molecular mechanism explaining the development of pheochromocytomas in the context of VHL disease.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **VHL Gene and Protein:** The VHL gene encodes the von Hippel-Lindau tumor suppressor protein (pVHL). pVHL is the recognition component of an E3 ubiquitin ligase complex.
- **HIF Pathway Regulation:** Under normal oxygen conditions (normoxia), prolyl hydroxylases (PHDs) hydroxylate specific proline residues on HIF-1α and HIF-2α. This hydroxylation allows the von Hippel-Lindau protein (pVHL) E3 ubiquitin ligase complex to bind to HIF and target it for ubiquitination and subsequent proteasomal degradation. Factor inhibiting HIF (FIH) hydroxylates an asparagine residue on HIF, preventing its interaction with transcriptional coactivators like CBP/p300.
- **VHL Mutation Effect:** Loss-of-function mutations in the VHL gene lead to a non-functional or absent pVHL protein. Without pVHL, HIF-1α and HIF-2α are not ubiquitinated and are therefore stabilized, accumulating in the cytoplasm and nucleus even under normoxic conditions. This mimics a state of hypoxia, hence the term "pseudohypoxia".
- **HIF Target Genes:** Stabilized HIF-1α/2α heterodimers translocate to the nucleus, bind to hypoxia response elements (HREs) in the promoter regions of target genes, and recruit transcriptional coactivators (CBP/p300). Key target genes include:
    - **Angiogenesis:** Vascular Endothelial Growth Factor (VEGF) promotes blood vessel formation, contributing to hemangioblastomas and renal cell carcinoma.
    - **Metabolic Adaptation:** Glycolytic enzymes (e.g., GLUT1, HK2, PFK) promote anaerobic glycolysis, even in the presence of oxygen (Warburg effect).
    - **Erythropoiesis:** Erythropoietin (EPO) stimulates red blood cell production.
    - **Catecholamine Synthesis:** HIF-1α can directly increase the transcription of genes involved in catecholamine synthesis, including Tyrosine Hydroxylase (TH), the rate-limiting enzyme. This contributes to the development and activity of pheochromocytomas.
- **Pheochromocytoma Development:** The stabilization of HIF-1α in VHL patients leads to increased expression of VEGF (promoting tumor angiogenesis) and TH (increasing catecholamine synthesis), both contributing to the development and functional activity of pheochromocytomas. The pseudohypoxic state also promotes metabolic reprogramming favoring tumor growth.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with classic signs and symptoms (headaches, visual changes, hypertension) and imaging findings (cerebellar hemangioblastoma, retinal angiomas, adrenal masses, renal cell carcinoma) consistent with VHL disease.
2. Genetic testing confirms a VHL mutation.
3. Elevated plasma free metanephrines confirm the presence of a pheochromocytoma.
4. The question asks for the molecular mechanism linking the VHL mutation to pheochromocytoma development.
5. VHL is a tumor suppressor gene whose protein product (pVHL) is part of an E3 ubiquitin ligase complex.
6. This complex targets hypoxia-inducible factors (HIF-1α and HIF-2α) for degradation under normal oxygen conditions.
7. Loss of functional pVHL leads to stabilization and accumulation of HIF-1α/2α, causing a pseudohypoxic state.
8. Stabilized HIF-1α/2α upregulates the expression of target genes, including VEGF (angiogenesis) and Tyrosine Hydroxylase (TH, catecholamine synthesis), contributing to tumor formation and function.
9. Option (A) accurately describes this mechanism: loss of pVHL leads to HIF-1α stabilization, promoting angiogenesis via VEGF and catecholamine synthesis via TH.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) [Analysis of why Option A is correct]:** This option correctly describes the central molecular mechanism of VHL disease. Loss of pVHL leads to HIF stabilization, which increases VEGF (angiogenesis) and TH (catecholamine synthesis), both contributing to pheochromocytoma development. This aligns perfectly with the known pathophysiology.
- **(B) [Analysis of why Option B is incorrect]:** While the PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway is involved in cell growth and survival in many cancers, it is not the primary mechanism directly linked to the VHL mutation and its effect on HIF. VHL's role is specifically in regulating HIF stability via ubiquitination.
- **(C) [Analysis of why Option C is incorrect]:** This option reverses the function of pVHL. pVHL normally *promotes* the degradation of HIF-1α. Loss of pVHL leads to *decreased* degradation (stabilization) of HIF-1α, not decreased expression of pVHL inhibiting TH.
- **(D) [Analysis of why Option D is incorrect]:** RET proto-oncogene mutations are associated with Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN 2), which can also cause pheochromocytomas, but this is a distinct genetic cause unrelated to the VHL pathway. VHL mutations do not directly cause increased RET expression.
- **(E) [Analysis of why Option E is incorrect]:** While impaired catecholamine degradation could theoretically contribute to tumor development, the primary mechanism in VHL-associated pheochromocytomas is the increased *synthesis* of catecholamines due to HIF-1α-mediated upregulation of TH, along with angiogenesis driven by VEGF. The main defect is not in degradation.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature             | VHL Disease Mechanism                                  |
|----------------------|-------------------------------------------------------|
| Gene Involved       | VHL (chromosome 3p25)                               |
| Protein Product     | pVHL (E3 ubiquitin ligase subunit)                     |
| Primary Defect      | Loss of pVHL function                                 |
| Key Pathway Affected| HIF-1α/2α degradation pathway                         |
| Consequence         | HIF-1α/2α stabilization (pseudohypoxia)               |
| Target Genes        | VEGF (angiogenesis), TH (catecholamine synthesis), EPO, glycolytic enzymes |
| Associated Tumors   | Hemangioblastoma, Renal Cell Carcinoma, Pheochromocytoma, Pancreatic neuroendocrine tumors, Endolymphatic sac tumors |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Loss-of-function mutations in the VHL tumor suppressor gene disrupt the ubiquitin-proteasome system by impairing the degradation of hypoxia-inducible factors (HIF-1α and HIF-2α). This leads to constitutive HIF activation, mimicking a hypoxic state (pseudohypoxia), which drives angiogenesis (via VEGF) and promotes the development of characteristic VHL-associated tumors, including pheochromocytomas (partially via increased TH expression).

### Question 125: Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1): Ras-GAP Defect & Adrenal Tumors
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1) is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by a loss-of-function mutation in the NF1 gene, leading to deficient neurofibromin (a Ras-GAP). This results in constitutive activation of the Ras signaling pathway, promoting cell proliferation and tumor formation, including pheochromocytoma, which can cause severe hypertension.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 32-year-old female presents with severe, refractory hypertension (BP 210/120 mmHg) despite being on three antihypertensive medications. Her past medical history is significant for multiple cutaneous neurofibromas, more than eight café-au-lait macules (>15 mm), and bilateral axillary freckling (Crowe sign). Slit-lamp examination reveals Lisch nodules (pigmented iris hamartomas). Laboratory investigations show elevated plasma free metanephrines (1800 pg/mL; normal < 150 pg/mL) and 24-hour urinary fractionated metanephrines (1450 mcg/24h; normal < 800 mcg/24h). Abdominal contrast-enhanced CT scan reveals a 6 cm left adrenal mass with heterogeneous enhancement. Given her clinical presentation and diagnostic findings, which of the following is the most likely underlying molecular mechanism contributing to the development of her adrenal tumor?

(A) Gain-of-function mutation in the RET proto-oncogene, leading to constitutive activation of tyrosine kinase receptor signaling.
(B) Loss-of-function mutation in the SDHB gene, impairing mitochondrial complex II function and succinate dehydrogenase activity.
(C) Loss-of-function mutation in the NF1 gene, resulting in decreased GTPase activity of p21-Ras.
(D) Gain-of-function mutation in the VHL gene, leading to increased degradation of hypoxia-inducible factor (HIF).
(E) Loss-of-function mutation in the MEN1 gene, causing decreased expression of menin, a tumor suppressor protein involved in transcriptional regulation.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 32-year-old female.
- **Presenting Complaint:** Severe, refractory hypertension.
- **Past Medical History:** Multiple cutaneous neurofibromas, >8 café-au-lait spots (>15 mm), bilateral axillary freckling (Crowe sign), Lisch nodules. These are classic diagnostic criteria for Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1).
- **Laboratory Findings:** Elevated plasma free metanephrines and 24-hour urinary fractionated metanephrines. These findings are highly suggestive of a catecholamine-secreting tumor, most commonly a pheochromocytoma or paraganglioma.
- **Imaging Findings:** 6 cm left adrenal mass with heterogeneous enhancement on CT scan, consistent with a pheochromocytoma.
- **Connecting the Dots:** The patient has clear clinical and diagnostic features of NF1 and biochemical/imaging evidence of a pheochromocytoma. The question asks for the underlying molecular mechanism linking NF1 to the development of this adrenal tumor.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **NF1 Pathophysiology:** NF1 is an autosomal dominant disorder caused by mutations in the NF1 gene located on chromosome 17q11.2. The NF1 gene encodes neurofibromin, a large protein with GTPase-activating protein (GAP) activity towards the small G protein p21-Ras. Neurofibromin normally acts as a tumor suppressor by promoting the hydrolysis of GTP bound to Ras, thereby inactivating Ras signaling. Loss-of-function mutations in NF1 lead to reduced neurofibromin levels or activity. This results in decreased GTP hydrolysis of Ras, leading to constitutively active Ras signaling. Activated Ras promotes cell proliferation, differentiation, and survival through downstream pathways like the MAPK/ERK pathway.
- **NF1 and Pheochromocytoma:** Patients with NF1 have an increased risk (1-5%) of developing pheochromocytoma, typically norepinephrine-secreting. The underlying mechanism is the constitutive activation of Ras signaling in chromaffin cells, driven by the loss of neurofibromin function, leading to uncontrolled cell growth and catecholamine production.
- **Other Options' Mechanisms:**
    - **(A) RET Proto-oncogene:** Gain-of-function mutations in RET are associated with Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 2 (MEN 2A and MEN 2B), which predisposes to medullary thyroid carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, and parathyroid hyperplasia (MEN 2A) or mucosal neuromas and marfanoid habitus (MEN 2B). The mechanism involves constitutive activation of the RET tyrosine kinase receptor. This is not the mechanism for NF1-associated pheochromocytoma.
    - **(B) SDHB Gene:** Loss-of-function mutations in SDHB (succinate dehydrogenase complex subunit B) are associated with paraganglioma type 2 (part of SDHx-related syndromes). SDHB is involved in mitochondrial complex II of the electron transport chain. Its loss leads to impaired energy metabolism and increased reactive oxygen species, contributing to tumor development. While SDHB mutations can cause pheochromocytoma/paraganglioma, this is not the mechanism in NF1.
    - **(D) VHL Gene:** Loss-of-function mutations in the VHL gene cause von Hippel-Lindau disease, predisposing to hemangioblastomas, renal cell carcinoma, pheochromocytoma, and pancreatic neuroendocrine tumors. VHL protein normally targets hypoxia-inducible factor (HIF) for degradation. Loss of VHL leads to HIF stabilization and increased expression of HIF target genes involved in angiogenesis and cell proliferation. This is not the mechanism in NF1.
    - **(E) MEN1 Gene:** Loss-of-function mutations in the MEN1 gene cause Multiple Endocrine Neoplasia type 1 (MEN 1), predisposing to pituitary adenomas, parathyroid hyperplasia/adenomas, and pancreatic neuroendocrine tumors. The MEN1 gene encodes menin, a tumor suppressor protein involved in transcriptional regulation. This is not the mechanism in NF1.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  Identify the patient's primary diagnosis based on clinical features: The constellation of multiple neurofibromas, café-au-lait spots, axillary freckling, and Lisch nodules strongly indicates Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1).
2.  Identify the cause of the patient's severe hypertension: Elevated plasma free metanephrines and urinary fractionated metanephrines, along with an adrenal mass on CT, confirm the presence of a catecholamine-secreting tumor, specifically a pheochromocytoma.
3.  Connect the primary diagnosis (NF1) to the secondary finding (pheochromocytoma): Recognize that NF1 is associated with an increased risk of pheochromocytoma.
4.  Determine the underlying molecular mechanism of NF1: Recall that NF1 is caused by mutations in the NF1 gene, leading to loss of neurofibromin function.
5.  Understand the function of neurofibromin: Neurofibromin acts as a GTPase-activating protein (GAP) for p21-Ras, promoting its inactivation.
6.  Deduce the consequence of neurofibromin loss: Loss of neurofibromin leads to decreased inactivation of Ras, resulting in constitutively active Ras signaling.
7.  Evaluate the provided options based on this understanding:
    - (A) RET mutations are linked to MEN 2, not NF1.
    - (B) SDHB mutations are linked to SDHx syndromes, not NF1.
    - (C) NF1 gene mutation leading to decreased Ras GTPase activity matches the known pathophysiology of NF1.
    - (D) VHL mutations are linked to VHL disease, not NF1.
    - (E) MEN1 mutations are linked to MEN 1, not NF1.
8.  Conclude that option (C) correctly describes the molecular mechanism underlying NF1 and its predisposition to tumors like pheochromocytoma.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Gain-of-function mutation in the RET proto-oncogene:** This mechanism is characteristic of MEN 2 syndromes, which are distinct genetic disorders from NF1. While MEN 2 also predisposes to pheochromocytoma, it is not the mechanism associated with NF1. The patient's clinical features (neurofibromas, café-au-lait spots, Lisch nodules) are diagnostic of NF1, not MEN 2.
- **(B) Loss-of-function mutation in the SDHB gene:** This is associated with SDHx-related syndromes, which can cause paragangliomas and pheochromocytomas. However, the patient's presentation is classic for NF1, not an SDHx syndrome. The underlying genetic defect in NF1 is different.
- **(D) Gain-of-function mutation in the VHL gene:** This statement is incorrect; VHL mutations are loss-of-function. Furthermore, VHL mutations cause von Hippel-Lindau disease, which predisposes to various tumors, including pheochromocytoma, but the mechanism involves HIF stabilization, not Ras activation, and the clinical picture is not consistent with VHL.
- **(E) Loss-of-function mutation in the MEN1 gene:** This causes MEN 1 syndrome, characterized by pituitary, parathyroid, and pancreatic tumors. It is unrelated to the clinical presentation of NF1 or the specific mechanism involving Ras signaling.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Genetic Syndrome | Gene Involved | Molecular Mechanism                                  | Associated Tumors                                                                                                | Key Clinical Features                                                                                                |
| :--------------- | :------------ | :--------------------------------------------------- | :-------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | :------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| NF1              | NF1           | Loss-of-function -> Decreased Ras-GAP activity -> Active Ras | Neurofibromas, Optic Gliomas, Pheochromocytoma, Malignant Peripheral Nerve Sheath Tumors (MPNST)                 | Café-au-lait spots, Neurofibromas, Lisch nodules, Axillary freckling                                                   |
| MEN 2A/2B        | RET           | Gain-of-function -> Constitutive RTK activation        | Medullary Thyroid Carcinoma, Pheochromocytoma, Parathyroid Hyperplasia (2A), Mucosal Neuromas (2B)                | Marfanoid habitus (2B), Mucosal neuromas (2B)                                                                         |
| VHL              | VHL           | Loss-of-function -> HIF stabilization                 | Hemangioblastomas (CNS, retina), Renal Cell Carcinoma, Pheochromocytoma, Pancreatic Neuroendocrine Tumors         | Hemangioblastomas, Renal cell carcinoma                                                                                |
| SDHx             | SDHB/C/D     | Loss-of-function -> Mitochondrial Complex II defect   | Paragangliomas, Pheochromocytomas, Renal Cell Carcinoma                                                            | Often presents younger, higher risk of malignancy (especially SDHB)                                                     |
| MEN 1            | MEN1          | Loss-of-function -> Decreased Menin (tumor suppressor) | Pituitary Adenomas, Parathyroid Hyperplasia/Adenomas, Pancreatic Neuroendocrine Tumors (Gastrinomas, Insulinomas) | 3 Ps (Pituitary, Parathyroid, Pancreas)                                                                               |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Neurofibromatosis Type 1 (NF1) is caused by mutations in the NF1 gene, leading to loss of neurofibromin function. Neurofibromin is a Ras-GAP, and its loss results in constitutive activation of the Ras signaling pathway, promoting tumor development, including pheochromocytoma. This highlights the importance of understanding the molecular basis of genetic syndromes and their associated tumor risks.

### Question 126: Adrenal Medullary & Catecholamine Physiological Mechanism
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Understanding the pathophysiology of paragangliomas associated with SDHx mutations, specifically the oncometabolite mechanism involving succinate accumulation, inhibition of prolyl hydroxylases (PHDs), stabilization of Hypoxia-Inducible Factor 1-alpha (HIF-1α), and its downstream effects on angiogenesis and tumor growth.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 24-year-old male presents to the emergency department with episodic headaches, palpitations, and diaphoresis. His blood pressure is 180/110 mmHg, and his heart rate is 110 bpm. He has a history of similar episodes over the past year. A CT scan of the abdomen reveals a 5 cm mass arising from the left adrenal gland, highly suggestive of a pheochromocytoma. Further investigation reveals the mass is extra-adrenal, located near the celiac axis, consistent with a paraganglioma. Biochemical testing shows elevated plasma free metanephrines (1500 pg/mL, normal < 150 pg/mL) and normetanephrines (1200 pg/mL, normal < 120 pg/mL). Genetic testing identifies a germline mutation in the SDHD gene. Which of the following pathophysiological mechanisms is MOST directly responsible for the increased risk of paraganglioma development in this patient?

(A) Loss of function of the SDHD protein leads to impaired oxidative phosphorylation, resulting in decreased ATP production and increased reliance on glycolysis, which promotes tumor growth.
(B) The SDHD mutation causes accumulation of succinate, which inhibits α-ketoglutarate-dependent dioxygenases, leading to stabilization of HIF-1α and increased expression of angiogenic factors like VEGF.
(C) The SDHD mutation results in the loss of a tumor suppressor gene, leading to uncontrolled cell proliferation through activation of the PI3K/AKT/mTOR pathway.
(D) The SDHD mutation causes increased production of reactive oxygen species (ROS), leading to DNA damage and genomic instability, promoting tumorigenesis.
(E) The SDHD mutation leads to decreased expression of the VMAT2 transporter, resulting in increased cytoplasmic catecholamines and enhanced autocrine stimulation of adrenergic receptors.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Young male with classic symptoms of catecholamine excess (headache, palpitations, diaphoresis) and hypertension.
- **Imaging:** Extra-adrenal mass near the celiac axis, consistent with a paraganglioma (a type of neuroendocrine tumor arising from paraganglia).
- **Biochemistry:** Markedly elevated plasma free metanephrines and normetanephrines confirm catecholamine excess, typical for pheochromocytoma/paraganglioma.
- **Genetics:** Germline mutation in the SDHD gene. This is the crucial clue linking the tumor type to a specific molecular mechanism. SDHD is a subunit of Complex II (Succinate Dehydrogenase) of the mitochondrial electron transport chain and the citric acid cycle.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST direct pathophysiological mechanism linking the SDHD mutation to paraganglioma development.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **SDH Complex Function:** Succinate Dehydrogenase (Complex II) catalyzes the oxidation of succinate to fumarate in the citric acid cycle and transfers electrons to ubiquinone in the electron transport chain. It is composed of four subunits: SDHA, SDHB, SDHC, and SDHD.
- **SDHx Mutations & Oncometabolites:** Germline mutations in SDHx genes (SDHA, SDHB, SDHC, SDHD) are associated with hereditary paraganglioma/pheochromocytoma syndromes. Loss-of-function mutations in these genes lead to impaired Complex II activity.
- **Succinate Accumulation:** Impaired Complex II function causes a buildup of its substrate, succinate, within the mitochondria.
- **Succinate as an Oncometabolite:** Succinate accumulates in the cytosol, where it acts as an oncometabolite. It competitively inhibits α-ketoglutarate-dependent dioxygenases.
- **Inhibition of Prolyl Hydroxylases (PHDs):** PHDs are enzymes that hydroxylate specific proline residues on HIF-1α under normoxic conditions. This hydroxylation is essential for HIF-1α recognition by the von Hippel-Lindau (VHL) tumor suppressor protein, leading to ubiquitination and proteasomal degradation of HIF-1α.
- **HIF-1α Stabilization:** By inhibiting PHDs, accumulated succinate prevents the hydroxylation of HIF-1α. This stabilizes HIF-1α, allowing it to translocate to the nucleus and heterodimerize with HIF-1β.
- **Transcriptional Activation:** The stabilized HIF-1α/HIF-1β complex binds to hypoxia response elements (HREs) in the promoter regions of target genes.
- **Downstream Effects:** HIF-1α activation leads to increased transcription of genes involved in angiogenesis (e.g., VEGF), glucose metabolism (e.g., GLUT1, HK2), erythropoiesis (e.g., EPO), and cell survival/proliferation. Increased VEGF promotes tumor vascularization, supporting tumor growth.
- **SDHD Specifics:** SDHD mutations are particularly associated with norepinephrine-secreting paragangliomas. The mechanism described above (succinate accumulation -> PHD inhibition -> HIF-1α stabilization) is the central pathway linking SDHx mutations to tumor development.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has a paraganglioma and a germline SDHD mutation.
2. SDHD is part of Complex II (Succinate Dehydrogenase).
3. Loss-of-function SDHD mutations impair Complex II activity.
4. Impaired Complex II leads to succinate accumulation.
5. Accumulated succinate acts as an oncometabolite, inhibiting α-ketoglutarate-dependent dioxygenases (PHDs).
6. Inhibition of PHDs stabilizes HIF-1α.
7. Stabilized HIF-1α promotes angiogenesis (via VEGF) and other processes that drive tumor growth.
8. Therefore, the mechanism directly responsible is succinate accumulation inhibiting PHDs, leading to HIF-1α stabilization. This corresponds to option (B).

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Impaired oxidative phosphorylation & glycolysis:** While SDH mutations do impair oxidative phosphorylation, the primary driver of tumorigenesis in this context is not simply decreased ATP production or a switch to glycolysis. The specific oncometabolite effect of succinate is the key mechanism. This option is partially true but misses the specific oncometabolite pathway.
- **(C) Loss of tumor suppressor function:** SDHx genes *do* encode subunits of a complex that has tumor suppressor functions, particularly SDHB and SDHD. However, the mechanism of tumor suppression is complex and involves mitochondrial respiration, iron-sulfur cluster biogenesis, and potentially direct protein-protein interactions. The *most direct* mechanism linked to SDHx mutations and paraganglioma development is the oncometabolite effect described in (B). While SDHD loss affects Complex II function, the *consequence* of that loss (succinate accumulation) is the direct trigger for HIF-1α stabilization.
- **(D) Increased ROS production:** Mitochondrial dysfunction can lead to increased ROS, but this is not considered the primary or most direct mechanism linking SDHx mutations specifically to paraganglioma formation via the oncometabolite pathway.
- **(E) Decreased VMAT2 expression:** VMAT2 (Vesicular Monoamine Transporter 2) is involved in packaging catecholamines into secretory vesicles. While alterations in catecholamine storage or release can occur in pheochromocytomas/paragangliomas, decreased VMAT2 expression is not the primary mechanism associated with SDHx mutations. The core issue is the genetic defect in SDH complex leading to the oncometabolite effect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature             | SDHx Mutations (e.g., SDHD) | Mechanism                                     | Consequence                                  | Associated Tumor(s)                  |
|----------------------|-----------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| Gene Involved       | SDHA, SDHB, SDHC, SDHD      | Loss-of-function                             | Succinate accumulation                       | Paraganglioma/Pheochromocytoma       |
| Enzyme Affected     | Complex II (SDH)            | Impaired Succinate Oxidation & ETC function | Oncometabolite (Succinate)                   |                                      |
| Target Enzyme       | α-Ketoglutarate Dioxygenases | Inhibition (e.g., PHDs)                       | HIF-1α stabilization                          |                                      |
| Transcription Factor| HIF-1α                       | Stabilization                                 | Increased VEGF, angiogenesis, tumor growth |                                      |
| Other Effects       | -                           | -                                             | Potential role in iron-sulfur cluster biogenesis | Renal cell carcinoma (SDHB), others |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Germline mutations in SDHx genes (SDHA, SDHB, SDHC, SDHD) cause hereditary paraganglioma/pheochromocytoma syndromes. The underlying mechanism involves impaired Complex II (Succinate Dehydrogenase) activity, leading to the accumulation of succinate. Succinate acts as an oncometabolite by inhibiting α-ketoglutarate-dependent dioxygenases, such as prolyl hydroxylases (PHDs), which normally degrade HIF-1α. Inhibition of PHDs stabilizes HIF-1α, promoting angiogenesis and tumor growth. This oncometabolite pathway is a key example of how metabolic alterations can drive cancer development.

### Question 127: [Adrenal Medullary & Catecholamine Physiological Mechanism / Genetic Pheochromocytoma Syndromes / SDHB Mutations]
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: This question tests the understanding of genotype-phenotype correlations in hereditary paraganglioma/pheochromocytoma syndromes, specifically the association between germline SDHB mutations and increased risk of malignancy, extra-adrenal localization, and distant metastasis.

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 31-year-old male with a family history of paragangliomas undergoes surgical resection of an 8 cm retroperitoneal paraganglioma discovered incidentally on abdominal CT imaging. Intraoperative blood pressure fluctuations are noted, but no hypertensive crisis occurs. Postoperatively, immunohistochemical analysis of the resected tumor reveals complete loss of SDHB protein expression. Two years later, routine surveillance imaging (PET/CT) reveals multiple new lesions, including osteolytic bone metastases and pulmonary nodules. Genetic testing confirms a heterozygous germline mutation in the SDHB gene. Which of the following statements regarding the clinical course and management of this patient is MOST accurate?

(A) The patient's SDHB mutation is associated with a low risk of malignancy, typically less than 5%, and the current metastatic disease is likely due to an unrelated primary malignancy.
(B) Patients with SDHB-mutated paragangliomas typically present with pheochromocytoma rather than paraganglioma, and the metastatic pattern is usually lymph node-based, not hematogenous.
(C) Germline SDHB mutations are associated with a significantly increased risk of malignancy (up to 40-50%) in paragangliomas, often presenting as extra-adrenal tumors, and characterized by a high propensity for distant hematogenous metastasis, as seen in this patient.
(D) The loss of SDHB protein expression in the tumor is inconsistent with a germline SDHB mutation; somatic mutations are the primary cause of SDHB loss in paragangliomas, and these are not associated with increased malignancy risk.
(E) While SDHB mutations increase malignancy risk, the metastatic pattern described (bone and lung) is atypical for SDHB-related paragangliomas, which more commonly metastasize to regional lymph nodes or the liver.

#### Correct Answer: C

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Profile:** 31-year-old male with a family history of paragangliomas, suggesting a hereditary syndrome.
- **Primary Tumor:** 8 cm retroperitoneal paraganglioma (extra-adrenal location).
- **Immunohistochemistry:** Complete loss of SDHB protein expression in the tumor. This is a crucial clue linking the tumor to a specific genetic mutation.
- **Clinical Course:** Development of distant metastases (bone, lung) two years after initial resection. This indicates malignant behavior.
- **Genetic Testing:** Confirmed heterozygous germline SDHB mutation.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST accurate statement regarding the clinical course and management, focusing on the implications of the SDHB mutation.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **Paragangliomas/Pheochromocytomas:** Tumors arising from chromaffin cells. Pheochromocytomas originate within the adrenal medulla, while paragangliomas arise from extra-adrenal chromaffin tissue (e.g., sympathetic ganglia along the sympathetic chain, parasympathetic ganglia near the organ of Zuckerkandl).
- **Genetics:** Approximately 30-40% of these tumors are hereditary. Key genes include RET (MEN 2A/2B), VHL (von Hippel-Lindau disease), NF1 (neurofibromatosis type 1), and SDHx genes (SDHB, SDHC, SDHD).
- **SDHx Genes:** Encode subunits of mitochondrial complex II (succinate dehydrogenase). SDHB, SDHC, and SDHD are located in the mitochondrial inner membrane and are essential for the electron transport chain and the Krebs cycle. SDHA, SDAC, and SDD are nuclear-encoded subunits.
- **SDHB Mutations:** Germline mutations in SDHB are the most common cause of hereditary paraganglioma/pheochromocytoma (accounting for ~10-15% of cases). They are associated with a particularly aggressive phenotype.
    - **Extra-adrenal Localization:** SDHB mutations are strongly associated with extra-adrenal paragangliomas (head, neck, abdomen).
    - **Malignancy Risk:** SDHB mutations confer the highest risk of malignancy among the hereditary paraganglioma/pheochromocytoma genes, estimated at 40-50%.
    - **Metastasis Pattern:** SDHB-related tumors have a high propensity for distant hematogenous metastasis, commonly to bone, lung, liver, and lymph nodes. Lymph node metastasis is also common.
    - **Age of Onset:** Often present at a younger age compared to sporadic or other hereditary forms.
    - **Bilateral/Multifocal:** Increased likelihood of bilateral or multifocal tumors.
    - **SDHB Protein Loss:** Loss of SDHB protein expression by immunohistochemistry is a characteristic feature of SDHB-mutated tumors, regardless of whether the mutation is germline or somatic. This loss is due to haploinsufficiency (having only one functional copy of the gene) or disruption of complex II assembly.
- **SDHD Mutations:** Also associated with increased malignancy risk, but generally considered slightly lower than SDHB. Often associated with gastric stromal tumors (GISTs) due to SDHD's role in regulating mTOR signaling.
- **VHL Mutations:** Associated with paragangliomas (often norepinephrine-secreting) and renal cell carcinoma. Malignancy risk is lower than SDHB.
- **RET Mutations:** Associated with MEN 2 syndromes, primarily causing medullary thyroid carcinoma and pheochromocytoma. Pheochromocytomas are typically adrenal and less likely to be malignant compared to SDHB-related tumors.
- **NF1 Mutations:** Associated with neurofibromas, optic gliomas, and pheochromocytomas. Pheochromocytomas are usually adrenal and have a lower malignancy risk than SDHB-related tumors.
- **Clinical Management:** Lifelong surveillance is crucial for patients with SDHB mutations due to the high risk of late recurrence and metastasis. Management of metastatic disease is challenging and often involves surgery, chemotherapy, targeted therapies (e.g., mTOR inhibitors like everolimus, sunitinib), and immunotherapy.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient has a paraganglioma (extra-adrenal).
2. The tumor shows loss of SDHB protein expression.
3. The patient has a confirmed germline SDHB mutation.
4. The patient developed distant metastases (bone, lung) after initial resection.
5. SDHB mutations are known to be associated with a high risk of malignancy (~40-50%) in paragangliomas/pheochromocytomas.
6. SDHB-related tumors frequently occur extra-adrenally.
7. SDHB-related tumors have a high propensity for distant hematogenous metastasis.
8. Therefore, the clinical course described (extra-adrenal tumor, SDHB loss, distant metastasis) is highly characteristic of an SDHB-mutated paraganglioma.
9. Evaluate the options based on this established association.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) [Incorrect]:** SDHB mutations are associated with a *high* risk of malignancy (40-50%), not low (<5%). The metastatic disease is directly attributable to the malignant nature of the SDHB-mutated paraganglioma.
- **(B) [Incorrect]:** SDHB mutations are associated with *paragangliomas* (often extra-adrenal) and pheochromocytomas (can be adrenal or extra-adrenal). The metastatic pattern is characteristically *hematogenous* (bone, lung, liver) and lymph node-based, not primarily lymph node-based.
- **(C) [Correct]:** This statement accurately reflects the known high malignancy risk (up to 40-50%), frequent extra-adrenal presentation, and propensity for distant hematogenous metastasis associated with germline SDHB mutations, consistent with the patient's clinical course.
- **(D) [Incorrect]:** Loss of SDHB protein expression is a hallmark of SDHB-mutated tumors, whether the mutation is germline or somatic. Germline mutations are the cause of the hereditary syndrome and increased malignancy risk in this patient. Somatic mutations can also occur but are less likely to be associated with the same high risk profile as germline mutations in the context of a family history and early onset.
- **(E) [Incorrect]:** While SDHB mutations increase malignancy risk, the described metastatic pattern (bone and lung) is *typical* for SDHB-related paragangliomas, not atypical. Lymph node metastasis is also common, but bone and lung are frequent sites of distant spread.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                 | SDHB Mutation              | VHL Mutation               | RET Mutation (MEN 2)      | NF1 Mutation               |
| :---------------------- | :------------------------- | :------------------------- | :----------------------- | :------------------------- |
| Tumor Type              | Paraganglioma/Pheo         | Paraganglioma/Pheo, RCC    | Pheochromocytoma         | Pheochromocytoma           |
| Location                | Extra-adrenal common       | Adrenal/Extra-adrenal      | Adrenal common           | Adrenal common           |
| Malignancy Risk         | Highest (~40-50%)          | Moderate (10-20%)          | Low (<10%)               | Low (<10%)               |
| Metastasis Pattern      | Hematogenous (bone, lung)   | Hematogenous/Lymphatic     | Lymphatic                | Lymphatic                |
| Other Associations      | GIST (SDHD)                | RCC, Hemangioblastoma      | MTC, Marfanoid habitus   | Neurofibromas, Optic Glioma |
| Age of Onset            | Younger                    | Variable                   | Variable                 | Variable                   |
| SDHB Protein Expression | Loss common                | Variable                   | Normal                   | Normal                   |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Germline mutations in the SDHB gene are strongly associated with hereditary paragangliomas and pheochromocytomas, conferring the highest risk of malignancy among the known genetic syndromes. These tumors often present extra-adrenally and exhibit a high propensity for distant hematogenous metastasis, particularly to bone and lung, necessitating lifelong surveillance. Loss of SDHB protein expression is a characteristic finding.

### Question 128: [Topic / Clinical Concept / Adrenal Medullary & Catecholamine Physiological Mechanism]
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Genomic imprinting of the SDHD gene, specifically maternal silencing and paternal inheritance pattern leading to hereditary paraganglioma-pheochromocytoma syndrome (HPGLD).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old woman is diagnosed with a heterozygous pathogenic mutation in the SDHD gene on chromosome 11q23. She is asymptomatic and has no personal history of paraganglioma or pheochromocytoma. She has two children, a son and a daughter, who are both phenotypically normal at the time of her diagnosis. Twenty-five years later, her son's child, a 20-year-old male, presents with bilateral carotid body paragangliomas discovered incidentally on neck imaging performed for unrelated symptoms. Genetic testing confirms the grandson inherited the same SDHD mutation from his father. The patient's daughter remains unaffected and has no children. Which of the following best explains the inheritance pattern of the SDHD mutation in this family?

(A) The SDHD mutation is inherited in an autosomal dominant pattern, and the grandson developed paragangliomas because his father was affected.
(B) The SDHD mutation is subject to genomic imprinting, where the maternal allele is silenced, and the grandson developed paragangliomas because he inherited the active paternal allele.
(C) The SDHD mutation is subject to genomic imprinting, where the paternal allele is silenced, and the grandson developed paragangliomas because he inherited the active maternal allele.
(D) The SDHD mutation is subject to incomplete penetrance, meaning not everyone who inherits the mutation develops symptoms, and the grandson's development is coincidental.
(E) The SDHD mutation is subject to anticipation, where the severity of the phenotype increases with successive generations, and the grandson's bilateral paragangliomas represent a more severe manifestation than his father's asymptomatic carrier status.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient:** 45-year-old female with SDHD mutation, asymptomatic.
- **Family History:** Two children (son and daughter), both phenotypically normal initially.
- **Grandson:** 20-year-old male, son of the patient's son, develops bilateral carotid body paragangliomas.
- **Inheritance:** Grandson inherited the SDHD mutation from his father.
- **Key Observation:** The grandmother (patient) has the mutation but is asymptomatic. Her son (the grandson's father) inherited the mutation but is asymptomatic. Only the grandson, who inherited the mutation from his father, developed paragangliomas. This pattern suggests a parent-of-origin effect.

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **SDHD Gene:** Encodes succinate dehydrogenase complex subunit B, part of the mitochondrial respiratory chain (Complex II). Mutations are associated with Hereditary Paraganglioma-Pheochromocytoma Syndrome (HPGLD), also known as SDHx-related paraganglioma/pheochromocytoma syndrome. SDHD mutations are the most common cause of HPGLD.
- **Genomic Imprinting:** An epigenetic phenomenon where certain genes are expressed in a parent-of-origin-specific manner. This means that the expression of the gene depends on whether it was inherited from the mother or the father. Imprinting involves DNA methylation and histone modifications that silence one of the alleles (either maternal or paternal).
- **SDHD Imprinting:** The SDHD gene on chromosome 11q23 exhibits genomic imprinting. Specifically, the maternal allele is normally silenced (imprinted) in somatic cells. This means that only the paternal allele is typically expressed.
- **Inheritance Pattern:**
    - **Maternal Transmission:** If a mother carries a pathogenic SDHD mutation, she will transmit either the mutated allele or the normal allele to her offspring. However, the maternal allele is silenced. Therefore, if she transmits the mutated maternal allele, the offspring will only have the normal paternal allele expressed, and they will be phenotypically unaffected (carrier). If she transmits the normal maternal allele, the offspring will have the normal maternal allele and the mutated paternal allele, but only the normal maternal allele will be expressed, making them phenotypically unaffected (carrier).
    - **Paternal Transmission:** If a father carries a pathogenic SDHD mutation, he will transmit either the mutated allele or the normal allele to his offspring. Since the paternal allele is not silenced, if he transmits the mutated paternal allele, the offspring will have an active mutated allele and will be at risk of developing paragangliomas/pheochromocytomas.
- **Clinical Manifestation:** HPGLD caused by SDHD mutations typically manifests clinically when the mutation is inherited from the father. Individuals inheriting the mutation from the mother are usually phenotypically unaffected carriers. However, male carriers who inherit the mutation from their father can transmit the active mutated allele to their offspring, leading to disease in the next generation.
- **In this case:** The grandmother (patient) has the SDHD mutation. She transmitted it to her son (the grandson's father). Since she transmitted the mutation maternally, her son inherited the mutation but remained phenotypically unaffected because his maternal SDHD allele was silenced. However, his son (the grandson) inherited the SDHD mutation paternally from his father. Since the paternal allele is active, the grandson developed paragangliomas.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1.  Identify the gene involved: SDHD mutation.
2.  Recognize the associated syndrome: Hereditary Paraganglioma-Pheochromocytoma Syndrome (HPGLD).
3.  Note the inheritance pattern: Grandmother has mutation, asymptomatic. Son inherited mutation, asymptomatic. Grandson inherited mutation from father, symptomatic (paragangliomas).
4.  Consider the parent-of-origin effect: The disease manifests only when the mutation is inherited paternally.
5.  Recall the mechanism: SDHD exhibits genomic imprinting, with maternal allele silencing.
6.  Apply the mechanism to the family: Grandmother (mother) transmits mutated maternal allele -> Son (father) is carrier, asymptomatic. Son (father) transmits mutated paternal allele -> Grandson is affected.
7.  Conclude: The inheritance pattern is consistent with genomic imprinting where the maternal SDHD allele is silenced.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Autosomal Dominant:** Incorrect. While SDHD mutations cause an autosomal dominant syndrome, the inheritance pattern here is not typical autosomal dominant because the grandmother and son (father) are carriers but asymptomatic, and the disease only manifests when inherited paternally. This points to a parent-of-origin effect, not simple autosomal dominance.
- **(B) Genomic Imprinting (Maternal Silencing):** Correct. This accurately describes the known mechanism for SDHD mutations. The maternal allele is silenced, so inheriting the mutation from the mother results in an unaffected carrier. Inheriting the mutation from the father results in an active mutated allele and potential disease development. This fits the family history perfectly.
- **(C) Genomic Imprinting (Paternal Silencing):** Incorrect. If the paternal allele were silenced, inheriting the mutation from the father would result in an unaffected carrier (only the maternal allele would be active). Inheriting the mutation from the mother would result in an affected individual (only the paternal allele would be active). This contradicts the observed pattern.
- **(D) Incomplete Penetrance:** Incorrect. While incomplete penetrance can occur in some genetic syndromes, the specific pattern here (grandmother and son asymptomatic, grandson affected after inheriting from father) strongly suggests a parent-of-origin effect (imprinting) rather than random incomplete penetrance. Imprinting provides a specific, mechanistic explanation for why the mutation manifests only in certain inheritance scenarios.
- **(E) Anticipation:** Incorrect. Anticipation refers to the worsening of a phenotype or earlier onset in successive generations, often seen in trinucleotide repeat disorders (e.g., Huntington's disease, myotonic dystrophy). This is not the mechanism for SDHD mutations, and the family history does not support anticipation. The grandson's disease is not necessarily a more severe manifestation; it's simply the manifestation of the active mutated allele inherited paternally.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)

| Feature             | SDHD Imprinting                                  |
| :------------------ | :----------------------------------------------- |
| Gene                | SDHD (Chromosome 11q23)                          |
| Syndrome            | Hereditary Paraganglioma-Pheochromocytoma (HPGLD) |
| Imprinting          | Yes                                              |
| Silenced Allele     | Maternal                                         |
| Active Allele       | Paternal                                         |
| Inheritance Pattern | Disease manifests when inherited paternally        |
| Carrier Status      | Inheriting from mother results in unaffected carrier |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Understanding genomic imprinting is crucial for interpreting inheritance patterns of certain genetic disorders. Specifically, SDHD mutations causing HPGLD demonstrate maternal imprinting, meaning the maternal allele is silenced. This leads to a unique inheritance pattern where disease manifests only when the mutation is inherited from the father, as the active paternal allele is then expressed. This contrasts with typical autosomal dominant inheritance.

### Question 129: [Adrenal Medullary & Catecholamine Physiological Mechanism]
- **Difficulty**: Hard
- **Core Concept / 重点考点**: Differentiating pheochromocytoma subtypes based on genetic mutations, biochemical profiles (catecholamine/metanephrine secretion patterns), and associated clinical features (e.g., pseudohypoxia, PNMT expression).

#### Clinical Vignette:
A 45-year-old male presents to the emergency department with episodic headaches, palpitations, and diaphoresis over the past year. His past medical history is unremarkable. Vital signs show a blood pressure of 185/110 mmHg, heart rate of 110 bpm, respiratory rate of 18 breaths/min, and temperature of 37°C. Physical examination reveals mild tremor and anxiety. Laboratory tests reveal elevated plasma free metanephrines (1800 pg/mL, normal <150 pg/mL) and normetanephrines (3500 pg/mL, normal <500 pg/mL). 24-hour urinary fractionated metanephrines and catecholamines are also markedly elevated. Genetic testing reveals a heterozygous mutation in the *SDHB* gene. Contrast-enhanced CT scan of the abdomen shows a large, highly vascular, extra-adrenal mass measuring 8 cm in diameter. The mass demonstrates heterogeneous enhancement. Given the genetic mutation and biochemical profile, which of the following is the MOST likely characteristic of this patient's tumor?

(A) Predominant secretion of epinephrine and metanephrines, high expression of phenylethanolamine N-methyltransferase (PNMT), and association with MEN 2 syndromes.
(B) Predominant secretion of norepinephrine and normetanephrines, low expression of PNMT, and association with pseudohypoxic signaling pathways.
(C) Predominant secretion of dopamine and 3-methoxytyramine, high expression of VMAT2, and association with neuroblastoma.
(D) Predominant secretion of epinephrine and metanephrines, high expression of PNMT, and association with RET proto-oncogene mutations.
(E) Predominant secretion of norepinephrine and normetanephrines, low expression of PNMT, and association with NF1 mutations.

#### Correct Answer: B

#### High-Yield Mechanism & Physiological Explanation:
##### 1. Clinical Vignette Breakdown & Key Clues (题干与题眼拆解)
- **Patient Presentation:** Episodic headaches, palpitations, diaphoresis are classic symptoms of catecholamine excess, strongly suggesting a pheochromocytoma or paraganglioma.
- **Vital Signs:** Hypertension (185/110 mmHg) and tachycardia (110 bpm) are consistent with sympathetic nervous system overactivity.
- **Laboratory Findings:** Markedly elevated plasma free metanephrines and normetanephrines confirm excessive catecholamine production and metabolism. Plasma free metanephrines are the most sensitive test due to continuous intratumoral metabolism.
- **Genetic Testing:** Heterozygous mutation in the *SDHB* gene is a crucial clue. *SDHB* is a subunit of succinate dehydrogenase (Complex II of the mitochondrial electron transport chain). Mutations in *SDHB* (and *VHL*, *FH*, *EPAS1*) are associated with Cluster 1 pheochromocytomas/paragangliomas.
- **Imaging:** Large, highly vascular, extra-adrenal mass is characteristic of paragangliomas, which are often associated with *SDHB* mutations. The heterogeneous enhancement suggests necrosis or cystic changes, common in larger tumors.
- **Question Stem:** Asks for the MOST likely characteristic of the tumor based on the genetic mutation (*SDHB*) and biochemical profile (elevated metanephrines/normetanephrines).

##### 2. Detailed Physiological Mechanism & Molecular Biophysics (肾上腺髓质生理机制与儿茶酚胺分子药理推导)
- **Pheochromocytoma/Paraganglioma Classification:** These tumors arise from chromaffin cells (pheochromocytoma in adrenal medulla, paraganglioma elsewhere). Recent genomic studies classify them into two main transcriptional clusters:
    - **Cluster 1:** Associated with mutations in genes involved in the hypoxia pathway (*VHL*, *SDHB*, *SDHC*, *SDHD*, *FH*, *EPAS1*). These tumors typically exhibit pseudohypoxic signaling (activation of HIF-1α), are often extra-adrenal, highly vascular, secrete predominantly norepinephrine (NE), have low expression of PNMT (phenylethanolamine N-methyltransferase, the enzyme converting NE to epinephrine), and are associated with succinate dehydrogenase deficiency. SDHB mutations are particularly common in extra-adrenal paragangliomas.
    - **Cluster 2:** Associated with mutations in genes involved in the Ras/MAPK and PI3K/Akt/mTOR pathways (*RET*, *NF1*, *MAX*, *TMEM127*). These tumors are typically adrenal, secrete predominantly epinephrine (EPI), have high PNMT expression, and are associated with syndromes like MEN 2 (RET) and neurofibromatosis type 1 (NF1).
- **Catecholamine Synthesis:** Tyrosine -> L-DOPA -> Dopamine -> Norepinephrine (NE) -> Epinephrine (EPI). PNMT converts NE to EPI within chromaffin granules.
- **Biochemical Profile:** The ratio of normetanephrine (NMT) to metanephrine (MT) in urine or plasma reflects the relative secretion of NE and EPI. A high NMT/MT ratio (>3:1) suggests predominant NE secretion, while a low ratio (<1:1) suggests predominant EPI secretion. The vignette states elevated metanephrines and normetanephrines, but the genetic finding (*SDHB*) strongly points towards Cluster 1 characteristics. Cluster 1 tumors typically have lower PNMT activity, leading to less EPI production and thus lower MT levels relative to NMT.
- **SDHB Mutation:** Loss of SDHB function impairs Complex II of the electron transport chain, leading to increased succinate levels, which can activate HIF-1α, mimicking hypoxia. This pseudohypoxic signaling promotes tumor growth and NE secretion. SDHB mutations are strongly linked to Cluster 1 features.

##### 3. Step-by-Step Clinical Deduction (临床逻辑逐步推理)
1. The patient presents with classic symptoms and signs of catecholamine excess (hypertension, tachycardia, headaches, palpitations, diaphoresis).
2. Elevated plasma free metanephrines and normetanephrines confirm the diagnosis of a catecholamine-secreting tumor (pheochromocytoma/paraganglioma).
3. The genetic finding of an *SDHB* mutation is the most critical piece of information.
4. *SDHB* mutations are characteristic of Cluster 1 pheochromocytomas/paragangliomas.
5. Cluster 1 tumors are known to exhibit pseudohypoxic signaling, are often extra-adrenal and highly vascular (consistent with imaging), and typically secrete predominantly norepinephrine due to low PNMT expression.
6. Therefore, the MOST likely characteristic of this patient's tumor is predominant secretion of norepinephrine and normetanephrines, low PNMT expression, and association with pseudohypoxic signaling pathways.

##### 4. Distractor Analysis (干扰项逐一深度剖析与鉴别)
- **(A) Predominant secretion of epinephrine and metanephrines, high expression of PNMT, and association with MEN 2 syndromes.** This describes Cluster 2 tumors, typically associated with *RET* mutations and MEN 2 syndromes, not *SDHB* mutations. Incorrect.
- **(B) Predominant secretion of norepinephrine and normetanephrines, low expression of PNMT, and association with pseudohypoxic signaling pathways.** This accurately describes the characteristics of Cluster 1 tumors associated with *SDHB* mutations. Correct.
- **(C) Predominant secretion of dopamine and 3-methoxytyramine, high expression of VMAT2, and association with neuroblastoma.** While dopamine can be secreted, predominant dopamine secretion is not typical for pheochromocytomas/paragangliomas. High VMAT2 expression is normal for chromaffin cells. Neuroblastoma is a pediatric tumor, not typically associated with *SDHB* mutations in adults. Incorrect.
- **(D) Predominant secretion of epinephrine and metanephrines, high expression of PNMT, and association with RET proto-oncogene mutations.** This describes Cluster 2 tumors associated with *RET* mutations (MEN 2), not *SDHB* mutations. Incorrect.
- **(E) Predominant secretion of norepinephrine and normetanephrines, low expression of PNMT, and association with NF1 mutations.** While norepinephrine secretion and low PNMT are characteristic of Cluster 1, *NF1* mutations are associated with Cluster 2 tumors, not *SDHB* mutations. Incorrect.

##### 5. High-Yield Summary Table (高分必备核心对比表)
| Feature                  | Cluster 1 (SDHB, VHL, FH) | Cluster 2 (RET, NF1) |
|--------------------------|---------------------------|-----------------------|
| **Genetic Mutations**    | SDHB, VHL, FH, EPAS1      | RET, NF1, MAX, TMEM127 |
| **Signaling Pathway**    | Pseudohypoxia (HIF-1α)    | Ras/MAPK, PI3K/Akt/mTOR |
| **Location**             | Often Extra-adrenal       | Primarily Adrenal     |
| **Vascularity**          | High                      | Variable              |
| **PNMT Expression**      | Low                       | High                  |
| **Primary Secretion**    | Norepinephrine (NMT > MT) | Epinephrine (MT ≈ NMT) |
| **Associated Syndromes** | SDHx syndromes            | MEN 2, NF1             |

#### Educational Objective / 核心考点总结:
Understanding the classification of pheochromocytomas/paragangliomas into Cluster 1 and Cluster 2 based on genetic mutations is crucial. Cluster 1 tumors (associated with *SDHB*, *VHL*, etc.) typically exhibit pseudohypoxic signaling, are often extra-adrenal, highly vascular, and secrete predominantly norepinephrine due to low PNMT activity. Cluster 2 tumors (associated with *RET*, *NF1*, etc.) are usually adrenal, secrete predominantly epinephrine, and have high PNMT activity. This classification helps predict tumor behavior and biochemical profiles.
